I. INTRODUCTION AND QUALIFICATIONS 
Q. 
Please state your name, affiliation, and business address. 
1 
A. 
My name is Joshua C. Nowak.  I am employed by Concentric Energy 
2 
Advisors, Inc. (“Concentric”) as Vice President. Concentric is a 
3 
management consulting and economic advisory firm, focused on the North 
4 
American energy and water industries. Based in Marlborough, 
5 
Massachusetts and Washington, D.C., Concentric specializes in regulatory 
6 
and litigation support, financial advisory services, energy market strategies, 
7 
market assessments, energy commodity contracting and procurement, 
8 
economic feasibility studies, and capital market analyses. My business 
9 
address is 293 Boston Post Road West, Suite 500, Marlborough, 
10 
Massachusetts 01752.

II. PURPOSE AND OVERVIEW OF TESTIMONY 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your Direct Testimony? 
20 
A. 
The purpose of my Direct Testimony is to present evidence and provide a 
21 
recommended range for the Companies’ cost of equity (sometimes referred 
22 


--- Page 5 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 3 of 73 
to as the Return on Equity or “ROE” for rate-setting purposes) and capital 
1 
structure for the Companies’ gas distribution utility operations.  My analysis 
2 
and recommendations are supported by the data presented in Exhibits __ 
3 
(JCN-1) through __ (JCN-8).

Q. 
Please provide a brief overview of the analyses that led to your ROE 
6 
recommendation. 
7 
A. 
As discussed in more detail in the remainder of my Direct Testimony, it is 
8 
important to consider the results of several analytical approaches in 
9 
determining a reasonable recommendation for the Companies’ ROE.  To 
10 
establish my ROE recommendation, I developed a proxy group that consists 
11 
of utilities that face investment risk generally comparable to that faced by 
12 
the Companies. I developed a multi-stage Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) 
13 
model and two forms of the Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”).  I have 
14 
presented the DCF and CAPM results weighted equally, and with the 
15 
Commission’s conventional 2/3 weighting of the DCF and 1/3 weighting of 
16 
the CAPM.  
17 
 
18 
The use of a multi-stage DCF model and two forms of the CAPM is 
19 
consistent with the approach employed by the Commission in prior cases.  
20 
While my equal weighting of the DCF and CAPM results does not conform 
21 
to the weighting typically employed in proceedings before the Commission 
22 
in the past, I explain in my Direct Testimony why placing less emphasis on 
23

Page 4 of 73 
the DCF model at this time is reasonable and consistent with the goals of 
1 
the Recommended Decision (“Generic Finance RD”) issued in the Generic 
2 
Finance Proceeding (“GFP”), Case 91-M-0509, which is the docket that has 
3 
been cited by the Commission to establish the ROE formula.  
4 
 
5 
In addition, I have considered how current market conditions have affected 
6 
the results of the ROE estimation models.  The current economic 
7 
environment is characterized by increased inflationary pressures.  As a 
8 
result of elevated inflation, the Federal Reserve’s responsive tightening of 
9

10 
evaluated the Companies’ risk profiles, including capital spending plans, 
11 
the regulatory environment, and the emerging risks due to decarbonization 
12 
policy.

Q. 
What are your conclusions regarding the appropriate cost of equity for 
3 
the Companies? 
4 
A. 
Based on the quantitative and qualitative analyses and the weighting of the 
5 
DCF and CAPM results presented in Figure 1, as well as my assessment of 
6 
the business and financial risk environment of KEDLI and KEDNY relative 
7 
to the proxy groups, I conclude that the appropriate ROE for the Companies 
8 
is within the range of 9.80 percent and 10.70 percent. The Companies are 
9 
requesting an ROE of 9.80 percent, which is at the low end of the range of 
10 
reasonableness and is a conservative estimate of the investor-required ROE.  
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please summarize your analysis of the appropriate ratemaking capital 
13 
structure for the Companies. 
14 
A. 
The analysis presented in Section VIII demonstrates that the Companies’ 
15 
requested equity ratio of 48 percent is below the mean equity ratio of 54.13 
16 
percent for the operating utility companies in the proxy group over the last 
17 
four years.  Therefore, I conclude that the Companies’ requested equity ratio 
18 
is reasonable, and in fact, conservative.  
19

III.  REGULATORY GUIDELINES AND FINANCIAL 
CONSIDERATIONS 
Q. 
Please describe the guiding principles to be used in establishing the cost 
22 
of capital for a regulated utility. 
23 
A. 
The foundations of public utility regulation require that utilities receive a 
24 
fair rate of return sufficient to attract needed capital to maintain service to 
25 
customers at reasonable rates.  The basic tenets of this regulatory doctrine 
26 


--- Page 9 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 7 of 73 
originate from two decisions by the United States Supreme Court, notably 
1 
Bluefield Waterworks and Improvement Company v. Public Service 
2 
Commission of West Virginia, 262 U.S. 679 (1923) (Bluefield), and Federal 
3 
Power Commission v. Hope Natural Gas Company, 320 U.S. 591 (1944) 
4 
(Hope).  In Bluefield, the Court stated:   
5 
A public utility is entitled to such rates as will permit it to 
6 
earn a return on the value of the property which it employs 
7 
for the convenience of the public equal to that generally 
8 
being made at the same time and in the same general part of 
9 
the country on investments in other business undertakings 
10 
which 
are 
attended 
by 
corresponding 
risks 
and 
11 
uncertainties… 
12 
The return should be reasonably sufficient to assure investor 
13 
confidence in the financial soundness of the utility and 
14 
should be adequate, under efficient and economical 
15 
management, to maintain and support its credit and enable it 
16 
to raise the money necessary for the proper discharge of its 
17 
public duties. 
18 
Later, in Hope, the Court established a standard for the ROE that remains 
19 
the guiding principle for ratemaking regulatory proceedings to this day: 
20 
[T]he return to the equity owner should be commensurate 
21 
with returns on investments in other enterprises having 
22 
corresponding risks.  That return, moreover, should be 
23 
sufficient to assure confidence in the financial integrity of 
24 
the enterprise, so as to maintain its credit and to attract 
25 
capital. 
26 
 
27 
Q. 
Please explain how these principles apply in the context of the regulated 
28 
rate of return.  
29 
A. 
Regulated utilities rely primarily on common stock and long-term debt to 
30 
finance permanent property, plant, and equipment.  The allowed rate of 
31 
return for a regulated utility is based on its weighted average cost of capital, 
32 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 8 of 73 
where the costs of the individual sources of capital (i.e., debt and equity) 
1 
are weighted by their respective book values.  The ROE represents the cost 
2 
of raising and retaining equity capital and is estimated with one or more 
3 
analytical techniques that use market data to quantify investor requirements 
4 
for equity returns.  However, the ROE cannot be derived mechanically 
5 
through quantitative metrics and models alone.  To properly estimate the 
6 
ROE, the financial, regulatory, and economic context must also be 
7 
considered. 
8 
 
9 
Based on the standards established in Hope and Bluefield, the 
10 
authorized ROE in this proceeding should provide the Companies with the 
11 
opportunity to earn a fair and reasonable return that is: 
12 
• Adequate to allow the Companies to attract the capital that is 
13 
necessary to provide safe and reliable service (the “capital attraction 
14 
standard”); 
15 
• Sufficient to ensure the Companies’ ability to maintain its financial 
16 
integrity (the “financial integrity standard”); and 
17 
• At a level that is comparable to returns required on investments of 
18 
similar risk (the “comparability standard”).  
19 
Importantly, a fair return must satisfy all three of these standards.  The 
20 
allowed ROE should enable the Companies to finance capital expenditures 
21 
on reasonable terms and provide the Companies with the ability to raise 
22 
capital under a full range of capital market circumstances to serve its 
23 


--- Page 11 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 9 of 73 
customers.  The DCF and CAPM approaches, while fundamental to the 
1 
ROE determination, are still only models.  The results of these models 
2 
cannot be mechanically applied without also using informed judgment to 
3 
consider economic and capital market conditions and the relative risk of 
4 
KEDNY and KEDLI as compared to the proxy group companies.  
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Did the Commission conduct a proceeding to review the standard for 
7 
estimating the cost of capital for a regulated utility? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  On August 21, 1991, the Commission established the GFP to review 
9 
the Commission’s then-current methodology for estimating the cost of 
10 
equity and to examine various alternatives.1 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please provide a brief summary of the purpose of the GFP. 
13 
A. 
The Commission’s goal in opening the GFP was to eliminate controversy 
14 
around ROE calculations and attempt to find common ground on 
15 
contentious issues by developing a consensus approach for setting utility 
16 
equity returns.  Among other things, the Commission questioned whether 
17 
there should be greater consistency in rate of return determinations from 
18 
company to company, such that differences in returns could be directly 
19 
attributed to differences in risk between companies, and whether the 
20 
 
1  
Case 91-M-0509, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Consider Financial and 
Regulatory Policies for New York State Utilities, Recommended Decision, (issued 
July 19, 1994) Generic Finance RD at 2. 


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 10 of 73 
Commission’s 
historical 
primary 
reliance 
on 
DCF-based 
ROE 
1 
determinations continued to provide fair returns.2     
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the conclusions outlined in the Generic Finance RD. 
4 
A. 
The Generic Finance RD concluded that the Commission should implement 
5 
a generic process for setting returns, based on proxy groups (not company-
6 
specific data), and that reliance on the DCF method should be replaced with 
7 
a combination of the DCF and CAPM methodologies.  The RD proposed to 
8 
use as a preferred convention a methodology that gives a respective 2/3rd to 
9 
1/3rd weighting to the results of the DCF and CAPM analyses. The RD 
10 
recognized that the CAPM “should figure prominently in the analysis” 
11 
because this methodology provides fundamental information on interest 
12 
rates and the returns required by stocks as a result of changes in interest 
13 
rates.  At that time, the CAPM was not accorded the same level of 
14 
prominence as the DCF analysis, given that the former had previously only 
15 
been used as a check.3  However, while the Generic Finance RD recognized 
16 
a benefit to establishing an “operating norm” with respect to setting the 
17 
ROE, it also recognized that there may be good reason to adjust either the 
18 
weightings of the DCF and CAPM models or to rely on different ROE 
19 
estimation models.  Specifically, the Generic Finance RD provides the 
20 
following guidance: 
21 
In either an annual proceeding to determine a rate of 
22 
return or in individual proceedings, the 2/3 DCF and 1/3 
23 
 
2  
Id. at 13-14. 
3  
Id. at 27. 


--- Page 13 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 11 of 73 
CAPM convention should be the presumption, but as 
1 
Multiple Intervenors suggests, parties would not be 
2 
barred from introducing new methods or different 
3 
weightings. Such parties, however, would have the 
4 
burden of convincing other parties and the Commission 
5 
of the relevance or superiority of their proposals. 4 
6 
To establish the “operating norm,” the Generic Finance RD recommended 
7 
specific forms of the ROE estimation models – a two-stage DCF approach 
8 
and a Traditional and Zero Beta CAPM. In the DCF model, the first-stage 
9 
growth was determined by the implied growth rate in Value Line dividend 
10 
forecasts for four- to six-years in the future.  The second growth rate began 
11 
with the end of the four- to six-year period of the first stage and extended 
12 
infinitely.  The second stage included what is termed an “SV”5 adjustment 
13 
for external growth through additional equity issuances.6  The CAPM result 
14 
was proposed to be based on the average of the Traditional and Zero-Beta 
15 
forms of the model.7  Dividend yields in the DCF analysis and risk-free 
16 
bond yields in the CAPM analyses were based on six months of yield data.8  
17 
Equity ratios were capped at the upper end of the levels necessary to 
18 
maintain an “A” bond rating.9  Although the Generic Finance RD was never 
19 
formally adopted by the Commission, it has served as a touchstone for the 
20 
Commission’s ROE determinations for more than 25 years. 
21 
 
4  
Id. 
5  
“SV” is the product of new shares issued as a percentage of current shares (“S”) and 
the value of the market-to-book ratio accruing to shareholders (“V”) as determined by: 
1 - (book value / market value). 
6  
Generic Finance RD at 21. 
7  
Id. at 24. 
8  
Id. at 26.

Q. 
Does the analysis presented in the remainder of your Direct Testimony 
1 
meet the intentions of the Generic Finance RD? 
2 
A. 
Yes, it does.  As discussed in greater detail in Section VI, the methodologies 
3 
that I have applied to estimate the return on equity are consistent with 
4 
Commission precedent since the issuance of the Generic Finance RD in the 
5 
GFP.  Moreover, the models used in my analysis extend the principles 
6 
advanced in the Generic Finance RD to best practices in financial analysis 
7 
and current capital market conditions, as was contemplated in the Generic 
8 
Finance RD.     
9 
 
10 
Specifically, I rely on the weighted results of DCF and CAPM analyses.  In 
11 
developing these ROE estimation models, I rely on proxy groups of risk-
12 
comparable companies as discussed in Section V.  I have used both the DCF 
13 
and CAPM methodologies to estimate the return on equity.  The multi-stage 
14 
DCF model that I relied on is consistent with the methodology that the 
15 
Commission has relied on in that it allows growth rates to vary over time.  
16 
Consistent with the fundamental principles upheld by the Commission, I 
17 
have applied two versions of the CAPM: Traditional and Zero Beta.  
18 
Finally, consistent with the principles of the GFP, to reduce the volatility 
19 
associated with the reliance on any one model, I have considered the DCF 
20 
and CAPM results weighted equally, and with the Generic Finance RD’s 
21 
proposed 2/3 weighting on the DCF and 1/3 weighting of the CAPM.    
22 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 13 of 73 
Q. 
Do the principles and intentions of the Generic Finance RD in the GFP 
1 
require adherence to a static formula? 
2 
A. 
No.  The GFP and the Generic Finance RD did not require strict adherence 
3 
to a static formula, rather, they promoted some basic principles and afforded 
4 
parties the flexibility to investigate approaches to address changing 
5 
financial market conditions.  When faced with the task of estimating the 
6 
cost of equity, analysts are inclined to gather and evaluate as much relevant 
7 
data (both quantitative and qualitative) as can be reasonably analyzed.  
8 
Analysts and academics understand that ROE models are tools to be used 
9 
in the ROE estimation process, and that strict adherence to any single 
10 
approach, or the specific results of any single approach, can lead to flawed 
11 
conclusions.  No model can exactly pinpoint the correct return on equity; 
12 
rather, each model brings its own perspective and set of inputs that inform 
13 
the estimate of ROE.  That position is consistent with the Hope finding that 
14 
“[u]nder the statutory standard of ‘just and reasonable,’ it is the result 
15 
reached, not the method employed, which is controlling.”10  
16 
 
17 
Although each model brings a different perspective and adds depth to the 
18 
analysis, each model also has its own set of inherent weaknesses and should 
19 
not be relied upon individually without corroboration from other 
20 
approaches.  Changes to inputs as a result of changes in economic 
21 
conditions could have widely different effects on the results of the various 
22 
 
10  Hope, 320 U.S. at 602. 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 14 of 73 
analyses.  Regardless of which analyses are performed to estimate the 
1 
investors’ required return on equity, the analyst must apply judgment to 
2 
assess the reasonableness of results and to determine the best weighting to 
3 
apply to results under prevailing capital market conditions.  No one model 
4 
can reliably and consistently estimate the cost of capital that meets the 
5 
fairness standard of Hope and Bluefield in all market conditions.  
6 
 
7 
Q. 
What is your conclusion with regard to regulatory guidelines and the 
8 
application of the models used to estimate the cost of equity? 
9 
A. 
The Generic Finance RD recognized that the CAPM provides fundamental 
10 
information on interest rates and the returns on stocks required by investors 
11 
as a result of changes in interest rates.  Further, it also recognized that there 
12 
may be good reason to adjust the weightings of the DCF and CAPM models 
13 
in the future.  As discussed in Section IV, interest rates have increased 
14 
substantially in recent months and are a particular concern to investors in 
15 
the current capital market environment.  Given the recent changes in the 
16 
financial markets, and the fact that the CAPM directly accounts for changes 
17 
in interest rates (i.e., risk-free rate) and the relative performance of utilities 
18 
relative to the broader equity market (i.e., Beta), it is necessary to evaluate 
19 
the appropriate weight to give the CAPM in determining the ROE for the 
20 
Companies. 
21 
 
22 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 15 of 73 
IV.   CAPITAL MARKET CONDITIONS 
Q. 
Why is it important to analyze capital market conditions? 
1 
A. 
The ROE estimation models rely on market data that are either specific to 
2 
the proxy group, in the case of the DCF model, or to the expectations of 
3 
market risk, in the case of the CAPM.  The results of the ROE estimation 
4 
models can be affected by prevailing market conditions at the time the 
5 
analysis is performed.  While the ROE that is established in a rate 
6 
proceeding is intended to be forward-looking, the analyst uses current and 
7 
projected market data, specifically stock prices, dividends, growth rates and 
8 
interest rates in the ROE estimation models to estimate the required return 
9 
for the subject company.   
10 
 
 
11 
As is discussed in the remainder of this section, analysts and regulatory 
12 
commissions have concluded that current market conditions have affected 
13 
the results of the ROE estimation models.  As a result, it is important to 
14 
consider the effect of these conditions on the ROE estimation models when 
15 
determining the appropriate range and recommended ROE for a future 
16 
period.  If investors do not expect historical or current market conditions to 
17 
be sustained in the future, it is possible that the ROE estimation models will 
18 
not provide an accurate estimate of investors’ required return during that 
19 
rate period.  Therefore, it is important to consider projected market data to 
20 
estimate the return for that forward-looking period. 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 16 of 73 
Q. 
Please describe the circumstances that have affected capital market 
1 
conditions. 
2 
A.  
Inflation and rising interest rates are the predominant factors in the current 
3 
economic environment.  However, it is important to put inflation and the 
4 
current monetary policy in historical context.  Following a period of 
5 
significant inflation and multiple recessions in the 1970s and early 1980s, 
6 
the past 30 years have been marked by low inflation and stable economic 
7 
growth as central banks have adopted inflation-targeting policies.  From the 
8 
mid-1980s until 2008, an extended period of low inflation was characterized 
9 
by tighter monetary policy as compared with the prior period.  In 2008, the 
10 
global financial crisis created a severe recession that was met with 
11 
comparatively looser monetary policy (i.e., lower target interest rates) and 
12 
significant fiscal policy stimulus.  However, inflationary pressures were 
13 
offset by the significant demand shock generated by the underlying credit 
14 
crisis.  As a consequence, the economy avoided significant inflation in the 
15 
period following the global financial crisis despite lower interest rates.  In 
16 
2018 and 2019, in the period immediately preceding the worldwide 
17 
pandemic of Coronavirus Disease 2019 (“COVID-19”), inflationary 
18 
pressures remained stable.  However, current capital market conditions have 
19 
been significantly affected by the economic repercussions of the COVID-
20 
19 pandemic and the subsequent fiscal and monetary policy reactions. 
21

Q. 
What steps did the Federal Reserve take to stabilize financial markets 
1 
and support the economy in response to COVID-19?  
2 
A.  
In response to the economic effects of COVID-19, the Federal Reserve 
3 
decreased the federal funds rate twice in March 2020, resulting in a target 
4 
range of 0.00 percent to 0.25 percent (which remained in effect until March 
5 
2022), and increased its holdings of both Treasury and mortgage-backed 
6 
securities, and supported increased credit to both businesses and individual 
7 
borrowers.  This increase in the money supply kept short-term interest rates 
8 
low and increased the ability of banks to lend to consumers and businesses.   
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please summarize the fiscal policy response from the U.S. Congress.  
11 
A.  
In addition to the Federal Reserve’s response, the U.S. Congress passed 
12 
approximately $4.5 trillion in fiscal stimulus programs.  On March 27, 
13 
2020, the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act was signed 
14 
into law, providing a large fiscal stimulus package aimed at mitigating the 
15 
economic effects of the coronavirus.  Further, in March 2021, the U.S. 
16 
Congress approved additional fiscal stimulus of $1.9 trillion in response to 
17 
the ongoing economic effects of COVID-19.  The extraordinary measures 
18 
taken by the Federal Reserve and U.S. Congress to support the economy 
19 
and stabilize financial markets impacted bond markets (deliberately driving 
20 
government and corporate yields lower) and equity markets (creating 
21 
upward pressure on valuations and downward pressure on yields for 
22 
dividend paying companies such as utilities).  However, these fiscal 
23 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 18 of 73 
stimulus programs have increased inflationary pressures, as evidenced in 
1 
the sharp run-up in inflation and corresponding upward spike in bond yields 
2 
since late November 2021. 
3 
  
4 
 Q.

5

Inflation has been a significant cause for concern among both policymakers 
6 
and investors.  As shown in Figure 2 below, inflation levels have moved 
7 
within a relatively narrow band over the past twenty years (other than during 
8 
the Great Recession of 2007/08).  Starting with the initial stages of the 
9 
COVID-19 pandemic in 2020, inflation levels have been driven higher, 
10 
reaching 9.1 percent in June of 2022.  Inflation is being driven by strong 
11 
consumer demand and supply constraints, some of which have been 
12 
exacerbated by COVID-19 related government stimulus programs in the 
13 
U.S. and international lockdowns.  More recently, the war in Ukraine, which 
14 
began in February 2022, is constraining the world’s supply of food and fuel, 
15 
pushing overall inflation higher and feeding into the cost of other products 
16 
and services. 
17

5

26

27

28

29

because no one knows exactly how high interest rates must go before

3

5

Looking forward, the inflation risks in the market are twofold: (1) either 
7

8

9

11

12

13

15

V.   PROXY GROUP SELECTION 
Q. 
Please explain why you have used a group of proxy companies to 
5 
determine the cost of equity for the Companies. 
6 
A. 
In these proceedings, we are focused on estimating the cost of equity for the 
7 
Companies’ rate-regulated, natural gas distribution utility operations in 
8 
New York.  Because ROE is a market-based concept and the Companies are 
9 
not publicly traded, it is necessary to establish a group of companies that 
10 
are both publicly traded and comparable to the Companies.  Even if the 
11 
Companies were publicly traded entities, it is possible that transitory events 
12 
could bias the Companies’ market value in one way or another in a given 
13 
period of time.  A significant benefit of using a proxy group is the ability to 
14 
mitigate the effects of short-term events that may be associated with any 
15 
specific company.  The proxy companies used in my ROE analyses possess 
16 
a set of business and operating characteristics similar to the Companies’ 
17 
utility operations, and thus provide a reasonable basis for estimating the 
18 
Companies’ ROE. 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 27 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 25 of 73

2

3 
to retail customers in the New York City boroughs of Brooklyn, Staten 
4 
Island, and Queens.17  KEDNY’s long-term issuer ratings are Baa2 
5

6 
(Fitch, Outlook: Stable).18

to retail customers in Long Island, New York and Queens, New York.19

KEDLI’s long-term issuer ratings are Baa1 (Moody’s, Outlook: Stable), 
12 
BBB+ (S&P, Outlook: Stable) and BBB+ (Fitch, Outlook: Stable).20  The 
13

14

20 
that practice, I began with the companies that Value Line classifies as

7 
unrelated to the overall cost of capital.

5

6

8

9

10

11 
 
21 See, e.g., Case 13-E-0030, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates,

from regulated operations instead of the percentage of total revenue derived 
1 
from regulated operations because net operating income is more 
2 
representative of the contribution of that business segment to earnings and 
3 
the corporation’s overall financial position.  Specifically, a significant 
4 
portion of gas and electric utility company revenue is derived from the costs 
5 
of purchased gas, purchased fuel, and purchased power, which, in most 
6 
cases, are recoverable through tracking mechanisms and do not, therefore, 
7 
contribute to earnings.  Furthermore, this portion of total revenue can 
8 
fluctuate considerably based on the cost of gas and other inputs.  Therefore, 
9 
relying exclusively on a revenue screen does not provide a clear or 
10 
necessarily consistent indicator of the contribution of the regulated utility 
11 
operations to a company’s earnings.  Net operating income excludes the cost 
12 
of purchased commodity and therefore more closely represents the 
13 
contribution of the business segment to earnings.

VI. COST OF EQUITY ESTIMATION 
Q. 
How is the required ROE determined? 
16 
A. 
While the cost of debt can be directly observed, the cost of equity and the 
17 
required ROE are market-based and, therefore, must be estimated based on 
18 
observable market information.  The required ROE is determined by using 
19 
one or more analytical techniques that rely on market data to quantify 
20 
investor expectations regarding the range of required equity returns.  
21 
Informed judgment is applied, based on the results of those analyses, to 
22 


--- Page 32 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 30 of 73 
determine where within the range of results the cost of equity for a company 
1 
falls.  As a general proposition, the key consideration in determining the 
2 
cost of equity is to ensure that the methodologies employed reasonably 
3 
reflect investors’ views of the financial markets, the proxy group 
4 
companies, and the subject company’s risk profile. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What methods did you use to determine the Companies’ cost of equity?  
7 
A. 
Consistent with Commission precedent, I used the DCF model and CAPM 
8 
approaches.  In establishing my recommended ROE, I relied on a multi-
9 
stage form of the DCF model, and, consistent with the Commission’s stated 
10 
preference, I used both the traditional form of the CAPM as well as the 
11 
Zero-Beta form of that model.  In both forms of the CAPM, I incorporated 
12 
a forward-looking measure of the Market Risk Premium.  
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Why do you believe it is important to use more than one analytical 
15 
approach? 
16 
A. 
Because the cost of equity is not directly observable, it must be estimated 
17 
based on both quantitative and qualitative information.  When faced with 
18 
the task of estimating the cost of equity, analysts and investors are inclined 
19 
to gather and evaluate as much relevant data as reasonably can be 
20 
analyzed.  As a result, a number of models have been developed to estimate 
21 
the cost of equity.  For that reason, I use multiple approaches to estimate the 
22 
cost of equity.  However, all of the models available for estimating the cost 
23 


--- Page 33 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 31 of 73 
of equity are subject to limiting assumptions or other methodological 
1 
constraints.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How are current market conditions affecting the results of the DCF and 
4 
CAPM models?  
5 
A. 
As discussed in Section IV, current capital market conditions are heavily 
6 
influenced by monetary policy aimed at mitigating inflationary pressures.  
7 
While long-term interest rates have increased substantially since the 
8 
historical lows of 2020, near-term conditions may not reflect investors long-
9 
term expectations.  Importantly, the risk-free rate in the CAPM analysis 
10 
should also consider projected yields on Treasury bonds, and the market 
11 
risk premium should be based on a forward-looking computation of the 
12 
expected return on the total market less the risk-free rate.  Market risk 
13 
premiums based on long-term historical averages are unresponsive to 
14 
movements in interest rates and would likely understate the market risk 
15 
premium and, accordingly, the cost of equity. 
16 
 
17 
Fluctuations in utility valuations impact the results of the DCF model.  The 
18 
dividend yield is calculated using historical average stock prices, which 
19 
may not fully reflect forward market expectations, especially as utility 
20 
valuations may not reflect the effect of recent increases in interest rates. 
21 
Long-term interest rates have increased substantially since the historical 
22 
lows of 2020 as the Federal Reserve continues to mitigate strong 
23 
inflationary pressures.  Importantly, the CAPM directly accounts for 
24 


--- Page 34 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 32 of 73 
changes in interest rates.   These circumstances collectively reinforce the 
1 
importance of using multiple models, as I have with the DCF and CAPM 
2 
approaches.  
3 
       
4 
A. DISCOUNTED CASH FLOW MODEL 
5 
 Q. 
Please describe the DCF approach. 
6 
A. 
The DCF approach is based on the theory that a stock’s current market price 
7 
represents the present value of all expected future cash flows.  In its most 
8 
general form, the DCF model is expressed as follows: 
9 
𝑃𝑃0 =
𝐷𝐷1
(1+𝑟𝑟)1 +
𝐷𝐷2
(1+𝑟𝑟)2 + ⋯+
𝐷𝐷𝑛𝑛
(1+𝑟𝑟)𝑛𝑛 
[1] 
10 
Where P0 represents the current market stock price, D1, D2, … Dn are all 
11 
expected future dividends, and r is the discount rate, or required ROE.  As 
12 
discussed below, I have not included the constant growth form of the DCF 
13 
model, but instead have focused on a multi-stage form of the DCF model.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please generally describe the DCF model you relied on.  
16 
A. 
The multi-stage DCF model is an extension of the constant growth form that 
17 
enables the analyst to specify growth rates over multiple stages.  As with 
18 
the constant growth form of the DCF model, the multi-stage form defines 
19 
the cost of equity as the discount rate that sets the current price equal to the 
20 
discounted value of future cash flows.  A multi-stage DCF model addresses 
21 
the possibility that mean five-year growth rates may not be reasonable in 
22 
perpetuity and that payout ratios could vary over time. 
23 


--- Page 35 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 33 of 73 
Q. 
Please describe the structure of the multi-stage DCF model.  
1 
A. 
The multi-stage DCF model that I have used sets the proxy company’s 
2 
current stock price equal to the present value of future cash flows received 
3 
over three time periods.  In all three periods, cash flows are equal to the 
4 
annual dividend payments that stockholders receive.  The first period is a 
5 
short-term growth period that consists of the first five years; the second 
6 
period is a transition period from the short-term growth rate to the long-term 
7 
growth rate that occurs over five years (i.e., years 6 through 10); and the 
8 
third period is a long-term growth period that begins in year 11 and 
9 
continues in perpetuity.  The ROE is then calculated as the rate of return 
10 
that results from the initial stock investment and the dividend payments over 
11 
the analytical period. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Has the Commission relied on a multi-stage DCF model in prior cases?  
14 
A. 
Yes, the Commission has relied on a two-stage form of the DCF model in 
15 
prior cases.22  The two-stage model that the Commission has relied on and 
16 
the multi-stage model that I rely on both define the cost of equity as the 
17 
discount rate that sets the current stock price equal to the discounted value 
18 
of future cash flows that are expressed as projected dividends.  Both models 
19 
project dividends using growth rates over multiple periods.   
20 
 
22 See Case 10-E-0362, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, 
Charges, Rules and Regulations of Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. for Electric 
Service, Order Establishing Rates for Electric Service, (issued June 17, 2011) (“2011 
O&R Rate Order”), at 68-69.   


--- Page 36 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 34 of 73 
Q. 
Is the multi-stage form of the DCF model consistent with the intent of 
1 
the two-stage model relied upon by the Commission? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  Both the construction of the multi-stage model and the underlying 
3 
assumptions are consistent with the two-stage model relied upon by the 
4 
Commission.  The constant growth DCF model assumes the expected 
5 
growth rate will be constant in perpetuity. The multi-stage forms of the DCF 
6 
model, including both the two-stage model that the Commission has relied 
7 
upon and the multi-stage form of the model that is relied on in my analysis, 
8 
recognize short and long-term growth prospects.   
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Does the multi-stage form of the DCF model offer improvements over 
11 
the two-stage model traditionally relied upon by the Commission? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The general form of the two-stage model relied upon by the 
13 
Commission involves a near-term growth stage based on projected 
14 
dividends and a long-term growth stage employing an estimated long-term 
15 
growth rate in dividends.23  The Commission’s application of a two-stage 
16 
DCF assumes that a company’s growth abruptly shifts to a long-run growth 
17 
state after the initial five-year period.  In contrast, the multi-stage model 
18 
relies on growth rates over three periods, as described above.  The multi-
19 
stage form of the DCF model provides for a gradual transition to a 
20 
 
23 See, e.g., Case 10-E-0362, Case 06-E-1433, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission

company’s expected long-term growth, whereas the two-stage DCF model 
1 
assumes the transition from short to long-term growth occurs in one year.  
2 
  
3 
Q. 
What market data did you use to calculate the current stock price in 
4 
your DCF model? 
5 
A. 
The stock prices that I relied on in my DCF model are based on the average 
6 
market closing prices for the proxy companies over the three months ended 
7 
March 31, 2023. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What growth rates did you rely on in the multi-stage DCF model? 
10 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (JCN-1), I began with the current annualized 
11 
dividend as of March 31, 2023 for each proxy group company.  In the first 
12 
stage of the model, the current annualized dividend is escalated based on 
13 
the average of the three-to five-year earnings growth estimates reported by 
14 
First Call, Zacks, and Value Line.  For the third stage of the model, I relied 
15 
on long-term projected growth in Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”).  The 
16 
second stage growth rate is a transition from the first stage growth rate to 
17 
the long-term growth rate on a geometric average basis. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Why do you believe that earnings growth rates are the appropriate 
20 
growth rates in the DCF model? 
21 
A. 
Earnings are the fundamental driver of a company’s ability to pay 
22 
dividends; therefore, earnings growth is the appropriate measure of a 
23 


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 36 of 73 
company’s long-term growth. In contrast, changes in a company’s dividend 
1 
payments are based on management decisions related to cash management 
2 
and other factors.  For example, a company may decide to retain certain 
3 
earnings rather than include those earnings in a dividend issuance.  
4 
Therefore, dividend growth rates are less likely than earnings growth rates 
5 
to reflect investor perceptions of a company’s growth prospects.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Is there support for the use of analysts’ earnings growth estimates in 
8 
the DCF model? 
9 
A. 
Yes, there is significant academic support for the use of analysts’ earnings 
10 
growth rates.  In addition, the majority of the data that are publicly available 
11 
to investors sets forth analysts’ projections of earnings growth rates.  Value 
12 
Line is the only publication I am aware of that provides projected dividend 
13 
growth rates. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please summarize the academic research on growth rates and stock 
16 
valuation.  
17 
A. 
The relationship between various growth rates and stock valuation metrics 
18 
has been the subject of much academic research.  Many published articles 
19 
specifically support the use of analysts’ earnings growth projections in the 
20 
DCF model in general, as well as for a method of calculating the expected 
21 
market risk premium.  While this article is focused on the calculation of the 
22 
CAPM, Dr. Robert Harris demonstrates that financial analysts rely on 
23

Q. 
Do you believe the Commission should adjust its historical reliance on 
1 
dividend per share growth rates during the initial five-year term of its 
2 
Two-Stage DCF?  
3 
A. 
Yes.   Sole reliance on Value Line projections of dividend per share growth 
4 
is not appropriate for several reasons.  First, the use of only dividend growth 
5 
rates ignores the substantial body of academic research demonstrating that 
6 
earnings growth rates are the most relevant factor in stock price valuation.34  
7 
Second, projections of dividend growth, which would not include growth in 
8 
retained earnings, only measure a portion of a company’s growth.  
9 
Therefore, earnings growth projections are more complete estimates of total 
10 
company growth than projected dividend growth rates.  Finally, Value 
11 
Line’s 4-6 year projections are not consensus estimates, but reflect the 
12 
viewpoint of a single analyst.  Therefore, the Commission’s models, which 
13 
have historically relied only on projected dividend per share growth rates 
14 
from Value Line, reflect the growth expectations of a single analyst in the 
15 
first stage of the model.  In contrast, there are several consensus estimates 
16 
of projected earnings per share growth rates that are publicly available and 
17 
widely used by investors, including Zacks Investment Research and First 
18 
Call.  Each of these consensus forecasts considers the growth expectations 
19 
for each company based on the expectations of multiple analysts.  It is not 
20 
reasonable to exclude these timely and widely available sources of 
21

Q. 
Why is the long-term GDP growth rate a reasonable estimate of long-
1 
term growth in the multi-stage DCF model? 
2 
A. 
Long-term estimates of GDP growth are commonly used in regulatory 
3 
proceedings as a proxy for the long-term growth rate in the multi-stage DCF 
4 
analysis.  That application is based on the common theoretical assumption 
5 
that, over the long-run, all companies in the economy will tend to grow at 
6 
the same constant rate.  That assumption is designed to address the 
7 
uncertainty associated with estimating individual company growth rates 
8 
over very long time horizons and is not meant to suggest that company 
9 
growth rates in the economy will indeed converge in practice over any given 
10 
period.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Is your calculation of GDP growth consistent with the way in which 
13 
other analysts’ compute estimates of long-term GDP growth? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  Investors understand that the U.S. economy goes through cycles of 
15 
growth and contraction.  Therefore, it is appropriate to consider the longest 
16 
period possible to measure historical real growth in GDP.  This view is 
17 
consistent with Morningstar’s explanation about measuring GDP growth: 
18 
Growth in real GDP (with only a few exceptions) has 
19 
been reasonably stable over time; therefore, its 
20 
historical performance is a good estimate of expected 
21 
long-term future performance.  By combining the 
22 
inflation estimate with the real growth rate estimate, a 
23 
long-term estimate of nominal growth is formed.39 
24 
 
25 
 
39  Ibbotson and Associates, Stocks, Bonds, Bills and Inflation, 1926-2012, 2013 
Valuation Yearbook, at 52. 


--- Page 44 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 42 of 73 
Furthermore, Morningstar supports the use of long-term historical data:  
1 
The 87-year period starting with 1926 is representative 
2 
of what can happen: it includes high and low returns, 
3 
volatile and quiet markets, war and peace, inflation and 
4 
deflation, and prosperity and depression.  Restricting 
5 
attention to a shorter historical period underestimates 
6 
the amount of change that could occur in a long future 
7 
period.  Finally, because historical event-types (not 
8 
specific events) tend to repeat themselves, long-run 
9 
capital market return studies can reveal a great deal 
10 
about the future.  Investors probably expect “unusual” 
11 
events to occur from time to time, and their return 
12 
expectations reflect this.40 
13

6.80 percent, which is considerably higher than the long-term growth

2 
 
3 
Q. 
How does your estimate of long-term growth differ from the estimate 
4 
the Commission has traditionally relied on? 
5 
A. 
The final stage of both the two-stage DCF model that the Commission has 
6 
relied on and my multi-stage DCF model extends into the future 
7 
indefinitely.  My long-term growth estimate reflects investors’ long-term 
8 
growth expectations for the period from 2032 through 2050.  Therefore, the 
9 
third stage of my multi-stage DCF model reflects investor growth 
10 
expectations beginning in the first year of the third stage of the model.  In 
11 
contrast, the growth estimate for the two-stage model that the Commission 
12 
has typically relied on is based on short-term growth rate forecasts.  The use 
13 
of the sustainable growth rate, calculated using Value Line’s published 
14 
projections, provides an estimate of growth four- to six-years in the future.  
15 
Relying on the sustainable growth rate in perpetuity in the second stage of 
16 
a two-stage DCF model does not provide a long-run estimate of growth.  
17 
Rather, the use of the sustainable growth rate assumes that the short-term 
18 
estimate for the four- to six-year period from the Value Line report date is 
19 
sustained in perpetuity.  
20 
 
21 
In contrast, the long-term growth rate in my DCF analyses reflects both 
22 
economic forecasts and market-derived projections of inflation over the 
23 
longest available time period (30 or more years).  Those estimates of long-
24 


--- Page 46 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 44 of 73 
term inflation expectations are combined with the long-term average 
1 
historical real GDP growth rate to calculate an expected nominal GDP 
2 
growth rate.  Consequently, the long-term growth estimate in my multi-
3 
stage DCF model represents investors and economists’ views of nominal 
4 
long-term GDP growth well beyond the time horizon reflected in the four- 
5 
to six-year Value Line sustainable growth estimate relied on by the 
6 
Commission in prior cases.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Are there other problems with the use of the sustainable growth rate as 
9 
an estimate of long-term growth? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  Using the sustainable growth rate to estimate the long-term growth of 
11 
a company uses a very narrowly-defined set of short-term projections based 
12 
on Value Line data.  Specifically, it relies on the following assumptions: (1) 
13 
projected dividends for year 2; (2) projected dividends for years 4-6; (3) 
14 
projected earnings for years 4-6; (4) projected book value for year 2; (5) 
15 
projected book value for years 4-6; (6) current estimate of actual 
16 
outstanding shares of stock; (7) projected shares of outstanding stock for 
17 
years 4-6; and (8) current three-month stock price.  Each of these 
18 
assumptions is estimated at most for six years into the future.  As defined 
19 
using these assumptions, the sustainable growth rate, which is applied over 
20 
the long-term in the Commission’s two-stage model, does not consider any 
21 
actual long-term forecasts for a specific company or the economy.  
22 
 
23 


--- Page 47 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 45 of 73 
Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding the methodology typically relied on 
1 
by the Commission to estimate the sustainable growth rate in the two-
2 
stage DCF model? 
3 
A. 
There are several reasons why the Commission’s current view of the 
4 
sustainable growth rate should not be continued in the two-stage DCF 
5 
model.  First, the sustainable growth rate is not a long-term measure of 
6 
growth and as such should not be applied in perpetuity in the second stage 
7 
of the model.  Second, the exclusive use of Value Line data, which is a 
8 
single analyst’s viewpoint, to establish the sustainable growth rate assumes 
9 
that investors do not consider any of the other financial information that is 
10 
widely available when establishing future dividend expectations.  In 
11 
addition, the sustainable growth rate calculation includes Value Line’s ROE 
12 
projections as an input, implicitly accepting them as reasonable.  However, 
13 
Value Line’s ROE projections are often significantly different from the 
14 
ROE estimates produced by the two-stage DCF model.  Finally, the 
15 
Commission’s sustainable growth rate methodology implicitly assumes that 
16 
investors establish long-term growth expectations based entirely on short-
17 
term, company-specific projections.  It is unreasonable to conclude that 
18 
investors would ignore the expectations of long-term macroeconomic 
19 
growth in establishing the long-term growth estimates for an electric or 
20 
natural gas distribution utility or any other company. 
21 
 
22

7

8

9

10

11 
understated.

B. CAPITAL ASSET PRICING MODEL  
14 
Q. 
Please briefly describe the Capital Asset Pricing Model. 
15 
A. 
The CAPM is a risk premium approach that estimates the cost of equity for 
16 
a given security as a function of a risk-free return plus a risk premium (to 
17 
compensate investors for the non-diversifiable or “systematic” risk of that 
18 
security).43  As shown in Equation [3], the CAPM is defined by four 
19 
components, each of which must theoretically be a forward-looking 
20 
estimate: 
21 
 
43  Systematic risks are fundamental market risks that reflect aggregate economic 
measures and therefore cannot be mitigated through diversification.  Unsystematic 
risks reflect company-specific risks that can be mitigated and ultimately eliminated 
through investments in a portfolio of companies and/or market sectors.

Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 47 of 73

1

2

3 
 
β = Beta coefficient of an individual security 
4 
 
rf = the risk-free rate of return 
5 
 
rm = the required return on the market as a whole 
6 
The term (rm – rf) represents the Market Risk Premium (“MRP”).  
7 
According to the theory underlying the CAPM, unsystematic risk can be 
8 
diversified away, therefore investors should be concerned only with 
9 
systematic or non-diversifiable risk.  Non-diversifiable risk is measured by 
10 
Beta, which is defined as:

15

16 
a specific security and the market reflects the extent to which the return on 
17 
that security will respond to a given change in the market return.  Thus, Beta 
18 
represents the risk that the selected security will not be effective in 
19 
diversifying systematic market risks.

--- Page 50 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 48 of 73 
Q. 
What risk-free rate did you use in your CAPM model? 
1 
A. 
I used three estimates of the yield on Treasury bonds: (1) the current three-
2 
month average yield on 30-year Treasury bonds (3.75 percent);44 (2) the 
3 
projected 30-year Treasury yield for 2023-2024 (3.88 percent);45 and (3) the 
4 
projected 30-year Treasury yield for the period 2024-2028 (3.90 percent).46  
5 
In determining the security most relevant to the application of the CAPM, 
6 
it is important to select the term (or maturity) that best matches the life of 
7 
the underlying investment.  As noted by Morningstar: 
8 
The traditional thinking regarding the time horizon of 
9 
the chosen Treasury security is that it should match the 
10 
time horizon of whatever is being valued…  Note that 
11 
the horizon is a function of the investment, not the 
12 
investor.  If an investor plans to hold stock in a company 
13 
for only five years, the yield on a five-year Treasury 
14 
note would not be appropriate since the company will 
15 
continue to exist beyond those five years.47 
16 
Because utility companies represent long-duration investments, it is 
17 
appropriate to use yields on long-term Treasury bonds as the risk-free rate 
18 
component of the CAPM.  In my view, the 30-year Treasury bond is the 
19 
appropriate security for that purpose.  Because the cost of capital is intended 
20 
to be forward-looking, it is appropriate to consider projected measures of 
21 
the market risk premium and interest rates. 
22 
 
23 
 
44 Bloomberg Professional. 
45 Aspen Publishers, Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, Vol. 42, No. 3 March 1, 2023, at 2. 
46 Aspen Publishers, Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, Vol. 41, No. 12 December 1, 2023, 
14. 
47 Morningstar Inc., Ibbotson SBBI 2013 Valuation Yearbook, at 44.

Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 49 of 73 
Q. 
Please describe your estimate of the market risk premium used in your 
1 
CAPM. 
2 
A. 
The estimated market risk premium is based on the expected return on the 
3 
S&P 500 Index, less the 30-year Treasury bond yield.  The expected return 
4 
on the S&P 500 Index is calculated using a DCF model for all companies 
5 
in the index based on market capitalization-weighted growth rates and 
6 
dividend yields.  The market risk premium implied by each of the three 
7 
Treasury yields discussed above is used in the CAPM analysis. 
8 
 
9 
The Commission previously has relied upon the calculation of a projected 
10 
market risk premium, based on the difference between the estimated 
11 
forward-looking required market return for the S&P 500, as provided by 
12 
BAML, and the risk-free rate.48  As a practical matter, that approach is 
13 
consistent with the Market DCF-derived forward-looking market risk 
14 
premium estimate discussed above (see also Exhibit __ (JCN-5). 
15 
Nonetheless, I have relied on the average of: (1) forward-looking required 
16 
market return for the S&P 500 as calculated using a DCF model for all 
17 
companies in the index; and (2) the estimated forward-looking required 
18 
market return for the S&P 500, as provided by BAML. 
19 
 
20 
 
48 See e.g., 2011 O&R Rate Order, at 77. 


--- Page 52 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 50 of 73 
Q. 
What Beta coefficient did you use in your CAPM model? 
1 
A. 
I considered the average Beta coefficients for the proxy group companies 
2 
as reported by Bloomberg and Value Line (see Exhibit __ (JCN-4)). The 
3 
Beta coefficients reported by Bloomberg were calculated using ten years of 
4 
weekly returns relative to the S&P 500 Index. Value Line’s calculation is 
5 
based on five years of weekly returns relative to the New York Stock 
6 
Exchange Composite Index. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Did you consider another form of the CAPM in your analysis? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  In prior proceedings, the Commission has relied upon the Zero-Beta 
10 
CAPM (the form of which is sometimes referred to as the “Empirical 
11 
CAPM”49) in estimating the cost of equity.  The Zero-Beta CAPM 
12 
calculates the product of the adjusted Beta coefficient and the market risk 
13 
premium and applies a weight of 75.00 percent to that result.  The model 
14 
then applies a 25.00 percent weight to the market risk premium, without any 
15 
effect from the Beta coefficient.  The results of the two calculations are 
16 
summed, along with the risk-free rate, to produce the Zero-Beta CAPM 
17 
result, as noted in Equation [4] below:   
18 
 
ke = rf + 0.75β(rm – rf) + 0.25(rm – rf)  
[4] 
19 
where: 
20 
 
ke = the required market ROE 
21 
 
β = Adjusted Beta coefficient of an individual security 
22 
 
49 See e.g., Roger A. Morin, New Regulatory Finance, Public Utilities Reports, Inc., 
2006, at 189.   


--- Page 53 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 51 of 73 
 
rf = the risk-free rate of return 
1 
 
rm = the required return on the market as a whole 
2 
 
3 
In essence, the Zero-Beta form of the CAPM addresses the tendency of the 
4 
“traditional” CAPM to underestimate the cost of equity for companies with 
5 
low Beta coefficients such as regulated utilities.  In that regard, the Zero-
6 
Beta CAPM is not redundant to the use of adjusted Betas; rather, it 
7 
recognizes the results of academic research indicating that the risk-return 
8 
relationship is different (in essence, flatter) than estimated by the CAPM, 
9 
and that the CAPM underestimates the “alpha,” or the constant return 
10 
term.50   
11 
 
12 
As with the CAPM, my application of the Zero-Beta CAPM uses the 
13 
forward-looking market risk premium estimates, the three yields on 30-year 
14 
Treasury securities noted earlier as the risk-free rate, and the Bloomberg 
15 
and Value Line Beta coefficients.  Exhibit __ (JCN-4) show the results of 
16 
the CAPM models.  The traditional CAPM model results range from 10.96 
17 
percent to 10.99 percent.  The Zero-Beta CAPM model results range from 
18 
11.29 percent to 11.31 percent.  The range established by the traditional 
19 
CAPM model and the Zero-Beta CAPM model results is 10.96 percent to 
20 
11.31 percent with a mean of 11.14 percent. 
21 
 
22 
 
50 Id. at 191.

Q. 
What was the reasoning for developing its weighting of the DCF and 
9 
CAPM methodologies in the Generic Finance RD? 
10 
A. 
At the time of the Generic Finance RD, the Commission did not have a 
11 
significant amount of experience with the CAPM.  The Generic Finance RD 
12 
noted that the Commission had historically used the CAPM as a check on 
13 
its DCF results, and was somewhat undecided as to “how far the 
14 
Commission should go in elevating the status of CAPM.”51   The Generic 
15 
Finance RD opted for a gradual transition towards the CAPM, ultimately 
16 
settling on a 1/3 weighting, indicating that “proposals have simply not 
17 
shown that the CAPM should be raised all at once to parity with the DCF 
18 
 
51  RD, at 27. 


--- Page 55 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 53 of 73 
analysis in the setting of returns on equity.”52  To the extent that this was a 
1 
consideration in the RD’s weighting determination, the Commission’s 25 
2 
years of experience with the CAPM since that time now provides a sound 
3 
basis for altering the weighting of the two ROE methodologies and raising 
4 
the CAPM “to parity with the DCF analysis....” 
5 
 
6 
Several regulators, including the FERC, have recognized the challenges 
7 
associated with relying solely on the DCF to establish the authorized ROE 
8 
for regulated utilities in recent years.  The FERC considered what it 
9 
termed “anomalous” market conditions as a basis for abandoning its 
10 
practice of sole reliance on the DCF model to set ROEs for electric 
11 
transmission companies which began in the 1980s. FERC reasoned: 
12 
The fact that utility stock prices appear to have 
13 
performed in a manner inconsistent with the theory 
14 
underlying the DCF methodology during the periods at 
15 
issue in these four complaint proceedings is an example 
16 
of what NETOs have described as “model risk” — the 
17 
risk that in some circumstances a model will produce 
18 
results that do not reflect real world experience.  It 
19 
appears that, for whatever the reason, investors during 
20 
this period have seen greater value in utility stocks than 
21 
the DCF methodology would predict.  This suggests that 
22 
the ROE estimated by that methodology may be 
23 
correspondingly inaccurate.53 
24 
 
52  Id. 
53  165 FERC ¶ 61,030, December 16, 2018, at 46. 


--- Page 56 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 54 of 73 
The importance of relying on multiple methodologies, while deemphasizing 
1 
the DCF, has been consistently recognized by a number of state 
2 
jurisdictions.54 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please summarize your conclusion regarding the relative weighting of 
5 
the CAPM and DCF results. 
6 
A. 
While the Generic Finance RD proposed the 2/3 weighting on the DCF, the 
7 
weightings and methodologies used to estimate the ROE were left open for 
8 
additional consideration in future rate proceedings.  Since then, the 
9 
Commission has employed the CAPM as one component of the formula 
10 
used to develop ROE estimates.  There does not appear to be any reason to 
11 
infer that the Commission has less confidence in the results of the CAPM 
12 
than those of the DCF.  The conditions that warranted the Commission’s 
13 
GFP inquiry and the subsequent Generic Finance RD in the early 1990s 
14 
exist again today with DCF results considerably lower than those from other 
15 
models, such as the CAPM, as well as returns authorized in other 
16 
jurisdictions.  Current capital market conditions are heavily influenced by 
17 
monetary policy aimed at mitigating inflationary pressures and 
18 
 
54  See, e.g., Pennsylvania Public Utility Commission, PPL Electric Utilities, R-2012-
2290597, meeting held December 5, 2012, at 80-81; State of Illinois Commerce 
Commission, Docket No. 16-0093, Illinois-American Water Company Initial Brief, 
August 31, 2016, at 10, Illinois Staff’s analysis and recommendation in that proceeding 
were based on its application of the multi-stage DCF model and the CAPM to a proxy

--- Page 57 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 55 of 73 
consequently long-term interest rates have increased substantially since the 
1 
historical lows of 2020.  Since the CAPM directly accounts for changes in 
2 
interest rates, it is appropriate to give the CAPM more weight as compared 
3 
to recent Commission decisions in the current capital market environment.  
4 
Therefore, it is reasonable to apply equal weighting to the DCF and CAPM 
5 
methods when determining the ROE for the Companies.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Are the assumptions used in the CAPM less reliable than the 
8 
assumptions used in the DCF model? 
9 
A. 
No.  As discussed previously, the CAPM relies on a risk-free rate, Beta and 
10 
the MRP.  The risk-free rate is readily observable and can be projected for 
11 
the forward-looking period.  Beta is estimated using the historical 
12 
relationship between the risk of the stock and the overall market.  Finally, 
13 
the market risk premium, while not observable, can be estimated for the 
14 
forward-looking period. My testimony discusses how the current economic 
15 
environment is directly affected by Federal Reserve policy and may not be 
16 
representative of forward-looking market conditions.  Therefore, while the 
17 
CAPM is sometimes criticized as relying on unobservable assumptions, the 
18 
ability to rely on forward-looking assumptions allows for a prospective 
19 
analysis of investors’ long-term expectations. 
20 
  
21

Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak

VII. 
  REGULATORY AND BUSINESS RISKS 
A. DECARBONIZATION POLICY 
1 
Q.  
Are there recent developments that have increased the business risk to 
2 
gas distribution utilities generally in New York? 
3 
A.  
Yes.   New York passed the Climate Leadership and Community Protection 
4 
Act (“CLCPA”), which envisions a net zero carbon economy by 2050.  As 
5

6 
Scoping Plan indicates that “a substantial reduction of fossil natural gas use 
7 
and strategic downsizing and decarbonization of the gas system”55 may be 
8 
required to achieve emissions limits.  This could result in significant 
9 
financial and market risk for the Companies.  While the implementation of 
10 
this legislation continues to be a work-in-progress, the uncertainty 
11 
surrounding it creates significant financial and market risk for utility 
12 
companies, especially utilities with natural gas distribution operations.   
13 
 
14

17 
commitment to significantly reduce greenhouse gas emissions poses long-
18 
term challenges.”56  In the most recent Moody’s report for KEDLI, this point

the proxy group companies: (1) test year convention; (2) fuel cost recovery;

C. REGULATORY ENVIRONMENT 
12 
Q. 
Please explain how the regulatory framework affects investors’ risk 
13 
assessments. 
14 
A. 
The ratemaking process is premised on the principle that, for investors and 
15 
companies to commit the capital needed to provide safe and reliable utility 
16 
services, the subject utility must have the opportunity to recover invested 
17 
capital and the market-required return on such capital.  Regulatory 
18 
commissions recognize that because utility operations are capital intensive, 
19 
 
62  Case 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310, Joint Proposal dated May 14, 2021, at 101 and 106-
107.

Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 62 of 73 
regulatory decisions should enable the utility to attract capital at reasonable 
1 
terms, which balance the long-term interests of investors and customers.  In 
2 
that respect, the regulatory framework in which a utility operates is one of 
3 
the most important factors considered in both debt and equity investors’ risk 
4 
assessments.  
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please explain how credit rating agencies consider the regulatory 
7 
framework in establishing a company’s credit rating.  
8 
A. 
S&P and Moody’s both consider the overall regulatory framework in 
9 
establishing credit ratings.  As shown in Figure 7, Moody’s establishes 
10 
credit ratings based on four key factors:

regulated utilities is based upon the regulatory environment.63  Moody’s

3 
“provide more granularity and transparency on the overall regulatory 
4 
environment, which is the most important consideration for this sector.”64 
5 
 
6 
With respect to the regulatory framework, Moody’s looks for transparency, 
7 
predictability, and supportiveness of regulatory commissions.65  For the 
8 
second factor, ability to recover costs and earn returns, Moody’s evaluates 
9

10

11

12

13

14 
sector.”67  This is particularly true as utilities are often cash flow negative 
15 
due to large capital expenditures, so any lack of timely recovery or 
16 
sufficiency of rates can strain access to capital markets. 
17

19 
stating, “we believe the fundamental regulatory environment in the

2 
A. 
Yes, they do.  From a credit perspective, the additional pressure on cash 
3 
flows associated with high levels of capital expenditures exerts 
4 
corresponding pressure on credit metrics and, therefore, credit ratings.  A 
5 
2016 S&P report noted: 
6 
When applicable, a jurisdiction's willingness to support 
7 
large capital projects with cash during construction is an 
8 
important aspect of our analysis. This is especially true 
9 
when the project represents a major addition to rate base 
10 
and entails long lead times and technological risks that 
11 
make it susceptible to construction delays. Broad 
12 
support for all capital spending is the most credit-
13 
sustaining. Support for only specific types of capital 
14 
spending, such as specific environmental projects or 
15 
system integrity plans, is less so, but still favorable for 
16 
creditors. Allowance of a cash return on construction 
17 
work-in-progress or similar ratemaking methods 
18 
historically were extraordinary measures for use in 
19 
unusual circumstances, but when construction costs are 
20 
rising, cash flow support could be crucial to maintain 
21 
credit quality through the spending program. Even more 
22 
favorable are those jurisdictions that present an 
23 
opportunity for a higher return on capital projects as an 
24 
incentive to investors.72  
25 
 
26 
Q. 
Have you conducted any analysis of the Companies’ projected capital 
27 
expenditures relative to the proxy companies? 
28 
A. 
Yes.  I compared the ratio of projected capital expenditures from 2024 
29 
through 2027 to net utility plant as of December 31, 2021, for KEDNY and 
30 
KEDLI with each of the proxy group companies.  Exhibit __ (JCN-7) shows 
31

1

2

3 
plant are higher than 17 of the proxy group companies (there are 19 
4

5

6

7 
proxy groups, which suggests that the Companies face greater risk from 
8 
their construction programs than the typical proxy group company.

Q. 
What are your conclusions regarding the effect of the projected capital 
1 
expenditure plans on the risk profiles of KEDNY and KEDLI and the 
2 
cost of equity? 
3 
A. 
It is clear that the Companies’ capital expenditure requirements as a 
4 
percentage of net utility plant are higher than the majority of the proxy 
5 
group companies.  This elevated level of capital expenditures relative to the 
6 
proxy group increases the importance of setting a return for KEDNY and 
7 
KEDLI that is within the range of reasonableness as established by the 
8 
returns for that group.  
9 
 
10 
VIII. CAPITAL STRUCTURE 
Q. 
Please summarize the Companies’ proposed capital structure.  
11 
A. 
As explained in Direct Testimony of the Capital Structure Panel, the 
12 
Companies are requesting a 48 percent equity ratio for ratemaking purposes, 
13 
which reflects a lower equity ratio than the Companies’ actual stand-alone 
14 
equity ratios.74 The requested equity ratio is consistent with recent 
15 
Commission precedent regarding the authorized capital structure for 
16 
utilities.75  
17 
 
74  Direct Testimony of Capital Structure Panel. 
75  “However, as Staff notes the 48% equity ratio is consistent with the equity ratio of the 
proxy group of companies with similar risk characteristics as St. Lawrence and the 
level that we have adopted for other New York utilities.”  Case 15-G-0382, St. 
Lawrence Gas Company, Inc., Order Establishing Multi-Year Rate Plan, issued July 
15, 2016, 2016 WL 3916059, at *15.  See, e.g., Case 19-E-0378 and 19-G-0379, New 
York State Electric & Gas Corporation, Order Approving Electric and Gas Rate Plans 
in Accord with Joint Proposal, with Modifications, issued November 25, 2020; Cases 
 


--- Page 71 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 69 of 73 
 
1 
Q. 
Have you conducted any analysis of the Companies’ proposed capital 
2 
structure as compared with the proxy companies? 
3 
A. 
Yes. I have reviewed KEDNY and KEDLI’s proposed capital structure as 
4 
compared with the actual capital structures of the operating companies in 
5 
the proxy group for the most recently reported four years.  As shown on 
6 
Exhibit __ (JCN-8), the mean annual equity ratio of the proxy companies 
7 
over that period is 54.13 percent.  
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What do you conclude from this analysis? 
10 
A. 
The requested 48 percent equity ratio is conservative considering the equity 
11 
ratios of the proxy companies and the current business and financial risks 
12 
of KEDNY and KEDLI, including significant capital investment programs, 
13 
credit metrics pressures and credit rating agency pressures.  This 
14 
information indicates that the utility operating subsidiaries owned by 
15 
holding companies with similar business characteristics to KEDNY and 
16 
KEDLI have for the last four years maintained average common equity 
17 
 
19-E-0380 and 19-G-0381, Rochester Gas and Electric Corporation, Order Approving 
Electric and Gas Rate Plans in Accord with Joint Proposal, with Modifications, issued 
November 25, 2020; Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0301, The Brooklyn Union Gas 
Company d/b/a National Grid NY, et al, Order Approving Joint Proposal, As Modified, 
and Imposing Additional Requirements, issued August 12, 2021);.  Note, however, that 
in a Central Hudson case the Commission allowed an equity ratio of 50%, stating: 
“Although the recommended ROE is somewhat higher than others we have recently 
approved for other utilities, interest rates and equity return requirements have generally 
increased since those proceedings were completed. Further, decreasing the common 
equity ratio in Rate Years 2 and 3 will reduce the overall cost of capital in those rate 
years.”  Case 20-E-0428, Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corp., Order Adopting Terms 
of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plan, issued November 18, 
2021, 2021 WL 5496045, at *21.  


--- Page 72 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 70 of 73 
ratios more than 6.00 percentage points above the 48.0 percent equity ratio 
1 
that the Commission approved for KEDNY and KEDLI in the Companies’ 
2 
last rate proceeding.  These higher proxy equity ratios reflect a level of 
3 
financial risk that is lower than the financial risk implied by the proposed 
4 
48.0 percent equity ratio.  In addition, equity ratios that are typically 
5 
authorized by the Commission for New York’s gas utilities (in 2020-2022 
6 
average authorized equity ratios in New York were 4.23 percentage points 
7 
below the national average for gas utilities),76 are considerably lower than 
8 
equity ratios authorized by other jurisdictions.  Therefore, I conclude that 
9 
the requested equity ratio should be considered a lower bound on the equity 
10 
ratio relative to the range of reasonableness established by industry practice.  
11 
As such, it would be reasonable for the Commission to use higher equity 
12 
ratios for KEDNY and KEDLI closer to those of the proxy group operating 
13 
companies for ratemaking purposes. 
14 
 
15 
IX.   MULTI-YEAR RATE PLAN 
Q. 
Would a multi-year rate plan impact your ROE recommendation?   
16 
A.  
Yes, it would.  The Commission has in many cases approved three-year rate 
17 
case settlements that often include stay-out premiums.  It is my 
18 
 
76  Source:  Regulatory Research Associates.  2020-2022 average authorized equity ratios 
for states other than New York (excluding limed issue riders) were 52.43 percent 
compared to an average authorized equity ratio of 48.20 percent in New York.  Rate 
cases in Arkansas, Florida, Indiana, and Michigan have been excluded from the 
analysis since the authorized capital structure approved in the cases includes deferred 
taxes and other credits as non-investor supplied capital. 


--- Page 73 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 71 of 73 
understanding that the Companies will provide four years of forecast data 
1 
in their rate filings.  In keeping with Commission precedent, a stay-out 
2 
premium would reflect the increased risk faced by the Companies under a 
3 
multi-year rate plan. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
What are the relevant risk factors to equity investors inherent in a 
6 
multi-year stay-out?  
7 
A. 
As described in Section IV, inflation and rising interest rates are the 
8 
predominant factors in the current economic environment.  As such, the 
9 
Companies, are exposed to changes in underlying costs that deviate from 
10 
expected levels of inflation. However, the stay-out premium associated with 
11 
a multi-year rate plan should not only compensate investors for changes in 
12 
the level of interest rates or inflation, but also for the potential risk of under-
13 
earning that is introduced by “staying out.”  By staying out of rate cases, the 
14 
utility may not fully recover material amounts of capital expenditures and 
15 
may be required to absorb losses due to differences between the cost of 
16 
service established in the rate plan and actual levels of revenue and expense.  
17 
The premium should compensate the utility and its investors for these 
18 
additional risks over and above interest rate risk.   
19 
 
20 
Q. 
What do you propose as the stay-out premium for a four-year rate 
21 
plan?  
22 
A. 
The ROE proposed by the Companies of 9.80 percent will not provide the 
23 
Companies a return commensurate with the return available on investments 
24 


--- Page 74 ---
Testimony of Joshua C. Nowak 
Page 72 of 73 
of similar risk over the term of the multi-year rate plan without an adequate 
1 
stay-out premium.  Consistent with prior cases in which a stay-out premium 
2 
was included in multi-year rate plans, I recommend that a stay-out premium 
3 
be included in a multi-year rate plan.  I believe 50 basis points is a 
4 
reasonable, albeit conservative, reflection of the incremental risk to the 
5 
Company under a multi-year stay-out provision.  
6 
  
7 
X.   CONCLUSION AND RECOMMENDATION 
Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding a fair return on book equity for 
8 
KEDNY and KEDLI? 
9 
A. 
My recommended return on book equity considers the results of the DCF 
10 
and CAPM models, summarized in Figure 9 (below), and the specific risks 
11 
to which the Companies are exposed.  I conclude that the appropriate ROE 
12 
for the Companies is within the range of 9.80 percent and 10.70 percent. 
13 
The Companies are requesting an ROE of 9.80 percent, which is at the low 
14 
end of this range of reasonableness and is a conservative estimate of the 
15 
investor-required ROE.  
16

JOSHUA C. NOWAK
