REPRESENTATIVE EXPERIENCE 
Expert Testimony and Litigation Support 
Mr. Nowak’s work includes regulatory project management, research, and analysis for expert witness 
testimony.  His work has included: 
•
Expert testimony on cost of capital, financial markets, return on equity, capital structure, and
debt financing issues
•
Regulatory strategy in return on equity proceedings, including coordination across several
utilities in joint-party proceedings
•
Extensive support for expert testimony in cost of capital and return on equity proceedings
through research, financial analysis, and testimony development
•
Expert testimony, sponsoring lead-lag studies, in support of utility cash working capital
requirements
•
Project management of expert testimony assignments, including all phases of the regulatory
schedule
•
Performing analysis to support expert testimony regarding affiliate expenses and allocations
Policy Analysis 
Mr. Nowak has contributed to projects related to policy review including: 
•
A review of natural gas capacity options and a cost-benefit analysis for state regulators seeking
to reduce energy costs for ratepayers
•
Analysis of the economic and environmental benefits of changes to natural gas
ratemaking/expansion policy
Mr. Nowak is a financial and economic consultant with more than fifteen years of experience 
in the energy industry.  He has provided expert testimony on regulatory issues in several 
proceedings before the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and regulatory commissions in 
Alaska, Connecticut, Kentucky, New Brunswick, New Hampshire, New York, Ohio, and Texas. 
Mr. Nowak specializes in providing rate case services on economic conditions and financial 
market matters related to the cost of capital.  He is also experienced in providing strategic 
direction on financing activities including bond offerings, credit rating analysis, and investor 
relations.  Previously, Josh was the Director of Regulatory Strategy & Integrated Analytics at 
National Grid where he was responsible for issues related to the cost of capital across its federal 
and state jurisdictional operating companies. He holds a Bachelor’s Degree in Economics and 
History from Boston College.

Bank of America Merrill Lynch: Quantitative Profiles 


--- Page 89 ---
Trading ideas and investment strategies discussed herein may give rise to significant risk and are 
not suitable for all investors. Investors should have experience in relevant markets and the financial 
resources to absorb any losses arising from applying these ideas or strategies. 
BofA Securities does and seeks to do business with issuers covered in its research 
reports. As a result, investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of 
interest that could affect the objectivity of this report. Investors should consider this 
report as only a single factor in making their investment decision. 
Refer to important disclosures on page 45 to 46.  
12499045 
  
Quantitative Profiles
2023: Value > Growth; Cash Flow based 
Value > Deep Value
 
Value won so far in 2022; stick with it… 
While Value factors (+5.1% on avg.) trailed Growth factors (+6.0% on avg.) in Nov., Value 
remains ahead of Growth so far in 2022 (-3.7% vs -4.0%, respectively). The Russell 1000 
Value Index leads the Russell 1000 Growth Index by 20ppt year-to-date (YTD). 2022 was 
marked by broad market leadership, as 73% of the almost 50 quantitative factors we 
follow outperformed the equal-weighted S&P 500 (a top-quintile year for market 
breadth in our data since 1987; Exhibit 49). All Value factors we follow except High EPS 
Yield (or Low P/E, -10.7% YTD) led the index. Historically, in prior years of strong market 
breadth (>73% of stocks outperforming, Value led Growth in the subsequent year in 67% 
of the cases and outperformed by a median of 1ppt. We see multiple reasons to stick 
with Value: 1) Growth remains stretched vs Value on most valuations metrics vs. history, 
2) Value remains deeply underweight by active managers (Exhibit 39), 3) During high
inflation periods Value led Growth 3-mth prior to Downturn (more below) and up to 12-
mth after, 4) During high inflation periods Value led Growth for 12mos after the Fed
stopped hiking, 5) Value fared well during periods of heightened rates volatility.
…but be selective 
Our US Regime Indicator edged lower in November (to 0.2 from 0.3, Exhibit 11), still in 
Late Cycle but approaching Downturn. Performance of Value in Downturns has been 
mixed: while deep Value (such as Low Trailing P/E, Low Forward P/E, Low Price to Book) 
underperformed, cash producing companies (our High Free Cash Flow to Enterprise 
Value (FCF/EV) and Low Price / Free Cash Flow (P/FCF) factors) led the index. Our DDM 
Alpha factor also fared well. These factors have also tended to outperform in “Late Late 
Cycle” phases like we are in today (Exhibit 3). Other factor groups for Downturns include 
High Quality, Low Risk and Large Cap, which historically outperformed with 71% to 86% 
success rate and about 5ppt avg. alpha (Exhibit 15) – though we see differences this 
cycle and expect small caps to outperform large (see SMID Year Ahead). 
Duration will likely remain under pressure 
While inflation has declined from recent highs, the market prices in additional 50bp of 
Fed hikes, even if achieved at a slower pace. Our High Duration factor, which includes 
secular growth companies with eventual payoffs far in the future and is sensitive to 
rising discount rates and cost of capital, was the second worst performing factor overall 
YTD, underperforming the index by 13.7ppt (second biggest margin in our history since 
1987). A continued rise in interest rates near-term (BofA Rates Strategists view) could 
continue to pressure High Duration stocks, especially given their elevated valuations. 
Dividends should become a bigger share of returns 
Dividend Yield was the third best factor overall YTD with a 17.1ppt lead over the index, 
its second best outperformance margin for the Jan – Nov period since 1987. Dividends’ 
contribution to total returns lagged over the last decade, but returning to historical 
norms would imply that >50% of total returns will be from income vs. 14% today (see 
US Equity Strategy Year Ahead). Demand for dividend paying stocks should remain 
elevated, especially given low valuations and low institutional ownership. See the 
Research Library for all data in Excel.

BofA Securities does and seeks to do business with issuers covered in its research

12506201 
 
Quantitative Profiles
Regime model hits a fork in the road: hard 
or soft landing?
 
Teetering at the brink of Late Cycle 
The US Regime Indicator inched lower in its Late Cycle phase, now just nine bp away 
from triggering a Downturn which is marked by poor and deteriorating macro conditions.
Of its eight inputs, four improved last month (the Earnings Revision Ratio, inflation, 10-
yr yields, and credit spreads) and four deteriorated (GDP forecasts, ISM PMI, Leading 
Indicators, Capacity Utilization). We ran scenarios for January (Exhibit 4) and found that 
if indicators flat-line, we would still hit a Downturn. But if indicators improve, which is 
suggested by the building narrative around a soft landing, we could avoid a Downturn - 
but we would need to see a marked improvement (10%+) to drive us back into Mid 
Cycle. For example, ISM PMI would need to jump to 53 from 48 etc. Since 1990, the 
Downturn phase occurred seven times, lasting an average of 12mths, ranging between 
six (9/95 – 3/96) and 19mths (GFC). If we extend the US Regime Indicator back to the 
70s and lost a few variables, the average downturn lasted 11mths. 
2022: “bird in hand” beat “buy the dream” 
Only a handful of factors yielded positive returns last year, with winners focused on 
“bird in the hand” strategies like high dividend yield, short equity duration, and positive 
earnings revisions. Value outperformed Growth (Russell 1000 Value beat Growth by 
22ppt in 2022), but absolute returns for all Value factors were negative. But one of the 
biggest pain trades was in expensive stocks (the bottom decile of Value factors) which 
lagged almost all other factors that we track - investors who stuck with expensive 
winners suffered most. Long equity duration stocks (“buy the dream” growth stocks that
are most susceptible to a rising discount rate) was among the worst five factors in ‘22. 
The reign of King Cash should continue 
All cash return strategies we track outperformed in 2022 as cash yields rose by 431bp. 
We expect this to continue: cash flow based valuation has an outperforming factor 
during most periods with one exception: the last decade’s zero interest rate policy (cash 
was worthless). We prefer companies generating cash rather than those that need 
capital to grow. Not only are rates likely to remain higher than they have been in recent 
past, but we are likely exiting an era of hyper-accommodative monetary policy. Factor 
positioning suggests that our view is contrarian: long duration stocks are still the most 
crowded and the most expensive of the factors we follow (Exhibit 15). 
Signs point to micro over macro in 2023 
2022’s market dominated by macro variables, where real rates explained more of stock 
returns than fundamentals, may be a thing of the past. Returns have shifted to 
fundamental drivers: reactions to Fed-speak pale in comparison to earnings surprises, 
valuation dispersion suggest plenty of mean reversion alpha. In 2023, we continue to 
focus on free cash flow-based valuation factors. This theme is still underweight but has 
historically provided protection during Downturns. And Risk and Small Size are deeply 
underweight and extremely inexpensive, but could prove to be good hedges against 
things going right, given that they tend to bounce back the most in recoveries. 
See the Research Library for all data in Excel.

Trading ideas and investment strategies discussed herein may give rise to significant risk and are 
not suitable for all investors. Investors should have experience in relevant markets and the financial 
resources to absorb any losses arising from applying these ideas or strategies. 
BofA Securities does and seeks to do business with issuers covered in its research 
reports. As a result, investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of 
interest that could affect the objectivity of this report. Investors should consider this 
report as only a single factor in making their investment decision.

12521562 
 
Quantitative Profiles
“Valuation doesn’t matter…”
 
“…price is the best predictor of price.” 
We hear this a lot, and the unwavering faith in price momentum investing is likely 
attributable to a liquidity-fueled decade that saw tremendous serial correlation across 
price returns. The average portfolio manager (~45 y.o.) has seen a financial crisis during 
which statistically cheap stocks were traps, followed by a decade during which value 
factors destroyed alpha almost every year while investing based on past price return 
turned in hefty alpha. The few value investors left see post-COVID shifts as a sort of 
come-uppance: Value (proxied by long-short EV/EBITDA) has returned 30 ppt since 
December 2020, whereas price return produced no alpha. And prior to the GFC, valuation
was a far better signal than basing future forecasts on past price returns (cover chart). 
Flirting with a downturn, but watch crosscurrents 
Our US Regime Indicator dipped its toe into Downturn territory this month; if we get a 2nd 
month of confirmation, we will officially be out of Late Cycle and in the abyss (Exhibit 5). But 
January saw market returns more similar to early cycle, as during a downturn, quality, low risk
and large caps outperformed most (Exhibit 9). Today’s economic crosscurrents suggest 
things may be a bit different (see Crosscurrents note). One rule that we think still holds: cash-
rich companies fare better, especially Free Cash Flow to Enterprise Value, a factor we like for
Late Cycle and Downturn phases (Exhibit 8). Value doesn’t always lag Growth during 
Downturns, as it led for 12m into the 70s’ Downturn (Exhibit 22). Downturns last about 12m 
on avg. based on data back to the 70s. 
Jan. effect on steroids: junk, small caps & positioning led 
And continued to lead into February. Risk factors (+13.3% avg) turned in best returns 
typical of January (see Jan effect note). Consistent with January (Exhibit 10), Low Quality
and Small Size which lagged in 2022 reversed in January. The 50 most shorted stocks in 
the S&P 500 bounced 14%, a two standard deviation move based on S&P 500 decile 
returns back to 1993, and short interest explained an unusually high percentage of 
returns variability in January (ρ=34%) the highest explanatory power in over 20yrs.  
Labor intensive stocks lag regardless of wage pressure 
The Fed is keenly focused on cooling wage inflation, a potential boon to labor intensive 
company’s margins. But labor intensive companies have been almost constant laggards 
relative to labor-light counterparts. Since ‘86, the former lagged the latter 70% of the 
time, trailing by ~2ppt p.a. (Exhibit 4). When CPI outpaced wage costs, performance was 
even worse, as underperformance margin was 7.4ppt p.a. (Data in Research Library.) 
Exhibit 2: Valuation matters, it just hasn’t for the last 10 years
